. Combining with (
157
), we conclude

\mathbb{P}_{g}(\widehat{g}\neq g)\;\leq\;\mathbb{P}_{g}(\mathcal{E}_{g}^{c})\;\leq\;\frac{1}{3},

and taking the supremum over
f\in\Theta
proves (
156
).
∎

Now let
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P\mid\mid Q)=\int\log\frac{dP}{dQ}dP
denote the Kullback-Leibler divergence between two distributions
P
and
Q
. We now introduce the following version of Fano’s inequality:

Lemma E.2

(Fano’s inequality)
.

Let
\Theta=\{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{M}\}
be a finite parameter set with
|\Theta|=M
. For each
\theta\in\Theta
, let
P_{\theta}
denote the distribution of the observations under parameter
\theta
. Suppose there exists a constant
0<\beta<\infty
such that
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{\theta}\,\|\,P_{\theta^{\prime}})\leq\beta
for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
. Then

\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\ \sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\ \mathbb{P}_{\theta}\!\left[\widehat{\theta}\neq\theta\right]\;\geq\;1-\frac{\beta+\log 2}{\log M}.

With
Lemma

E.1
and
Lemma

E.2
, obtaining a minimax lower bound on the regret reduces to finding a "hard" subset
\Theta\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{p,q}(\mathbb{V}_{T})
such that we can lower bound
\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}g,\tilde{g}\in\Theta\end{subarray}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\chi(g_{t},\tilde{g}_{t})
and upper bound
\sup_{g,\widetilde{g}\in\Theta}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{g}\,\|\,P_{\widetilde{g}})
. We proceed by constructing two
\mu
-strongly convex,
\mathcal{O}(\mu)
-smooth losses
g_{+}
and
g_{-}
whose minimizers are separated by
2a
along
e_{1}
, but whose gradients differ only inside a ball of radius
\asymp a
. This localization makes
\|\nabla g_{+}-\nabla g_{-}\|_{L^{p}(\Theta)}
of order
\mu a^{1+d/p}
so we can switch between
g_{+}
and
g_{-}
many times while staying within the gradient-variation budget. We then build a
J
-block family of sequences
G_{1:T}^{u}
indexed by
u\in\{\pm 1\}^{J}
where each block uses either
g_{+}
or
g_{-}
and
\Delta_{T}:=\lfloor T/J\rfloor
for
1\leq J\leq T
to be determined later. If an algorithm cannot reliably infer
u
from the noisy gradients, it must play near the wrong minimizer on a constant fraction of the time indices, costing
\Omega(\mu a^{2})
loss per round. Hence the regret is
\Omega(\mu a^{2}T)
times the testing error. Finally we can upper bound the mutual information using Gaussian KL chain rule, apply Fano’s inequality, and choose
\Delta_{T}
to conclude.

E.2
Constructing hard smooth convex functions with localized gradients

We will proceed with constructing hard
\mu
-strongly convex,
\mathcal{O}(\mu)
-smooth losses
g_{+}
and
g_{-}
. Fix a smooth bump
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow[0,1]
such that

\psi(\bm{\theta})=1
for
\|\bm{\theta}\|\leq 1/2
and
\psi(\bm{\theta})=0
for
\|\bm{\theta}\|\geq 1
with
\|\nabla\psi(\bm{\theta})\|\leq C_{\psi}
and
\|\nabla^{2}\psi(\bm{\theta})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C_{\psi}
for all
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
. For scale
r>0
, define
\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta}):=\psi(\bm{\theta}/r)
. Then we have that
\|\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})\|\leq C_{\psi}/r
and
\|\nabla^{2}\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C_{\psi}/r^{2}
. Let
e_{1}=(1,0,\dots,0)
. Choose parameters
a>0
and
r>0
with
a\leq r/4
. Define on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
the following function:

g_{u}(\bm{\theta}):=\frac{\mu}{2}\|\bm{\theta}\|^{2}-u\cdot\mu a\langle\bm{\theta},e_{1}\rangle\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta}),\;\;\;u\in\{+1,-1\}.

(163)

We now show that this function is strongly convex and smooth on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Lemma E.3

(Smoothness and strong convexity of
g_{u}
)
.

There exists a universal constant
c_{1}>0
such that if
a/r\leq c_{1}
, then each
g_{u}
is
\mu/4
strongly convex and
2\mu
-smooth on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof of
Lemma

E.3
.

We can compute the gradient as follows:

\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}g_{u}(\bm{\theta})=\mu\bm{\theta}-u\cdot\mu a\left(e_{1}\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})+\langle\bm{\theta},e_{1}\rangle\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})\right).

(164)

Similarly we can compute the Hessian as:

\nabla^{2}g_{u}(\bm{\theta})=\mu I_{d}-u\cdot\mu a\left(e_{1}(\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta}))^{\top}+(\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta}))e_{1}^{\top}+\langle\bm{\theta},e_{1}\rangle\nabla^{2}\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})\right).

(165)

Using
\|e_{1}(\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta}))^{\top}\|\leq\|\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})\|
, the derivative bounds for the bump function, and
\left\lvert\langle\bm{\theta},e_{1}\rangle\right\rvert\leq\|\bm{\theta}\|\leq r
whenever
\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})\neq 0
, we get for all
\theta\in\Theta
:

\|\nabla^{2}g_{u}(\bm{\theta})-\mu I_{d}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\mu a\left(2\|\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta}\|+\left\lvert\langle\bm{\theta},e_{1}\rangle\right\rvert\|\nabla^{2}\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta}\|_{\mathrm{op}})\right)\leq\frac{3\mu aC_{\psi}}{r}.

(166)

So if
a/r\leq c_{1}:=1/(12C_{\psi})
, we have
\|\nabla^{2}g_{u}(\bm{\theta})-\mu I_{d}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\mu/4
. This completes the proof.
∎

We now show that with this construction, the discrepancy between these functions is lower bounded by
\mu a^{2}
.

Lemma E.4

(Minimizers and discrepancy of the two-point construction)
.

Assume
a\leq r/4
and
a/r\leq c_{1}
, where
c_{1}>0
is the constant from
Lemma

E.3
. Let
g_{u}
be defined in
Equation

163
for
u\in\{+1,-1\}
, and assume that
\Theta
contains the line segment
\{tae_{1}:t\in[-1,1]\}
.

1.

(
Minimizers.
) Each
g_{u}
admits a unique minimizer
\bm{\theta}_{u}^{\star}
, and
\bm{\theta}_{u}^{\star}=uae_{1}
.

2.

(
Discrepancy.
) The two-point discrepancy satisfies
\chi(g_{+},g_{-})\;\geq\;\mu a^{2}/8
.

Proof of
Lemma

E.4
.

On the Euclidean ball
\{\bm{\theta}:\|\bm{\theta}\|_{2}\leq r/2\}
, we have
\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})=1
and
\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})=0
by construction of the bump.
Therefore, for all
\|\bm{\theta}\|_{2}\leq r/2
,
\nabla g_{u}(\bm{\theta})=\mu\bm{\theta}-u\mu ae_{1}
. Setting
\nabla g_{u}(\bm{\theta})=0
yields
\bm{\theta}=uae_{1}
, and this point indeed lies in the region where the above simplification holds since

\|uae_{1}\|_{2}=a\leq r/4<r/2
. By
Lemma

E.3
, each
g_{u}
is
\mu/4
–strongly convex on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, hence admitting a unique global minimizer, which must coincide with its unique stationary point. Thus
\bm{\theta}_{u}^{\star}=uae_{1}
and this proves the first claim.

To prove the second claim, by
\mu/4
–strong convexity of
g_{u}
, for every
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
,

g_{u}(\bm{\theta})-g_{u}(\bm{\theta}_{u}^{\star})\;\geq\;\frac{\mu}{8}\,\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}_{u}^{\star}\|_{2}^{2}.

Define
d_{+}(\bm{\theta}):=\|\bm{\theta}-ae_{1}\|_{2}
and
d_{-}(\bm{\theta}):=\|\bm{\theta}+ae_{1}\|_{2}
. Then

\max\{d_{+}(\bm{\theta})^{2},d_{-}(\bm{\theta})^{2}\}\;\geq\;\frac{1}{2}\big(d_{+}(\bm{\theta})^{2}+d_{-}(\bm{\theta})^{2}\big).

Moreover, by the parallelogram identity,

d_{+}(\bm{\theta})^{2}+d_{-}(\bm{\theta})^{2}=\|\bm{\theta}-ae_{1}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\bm{\theta}+ae_{1}\|_{2}^{2}=2\|\bm{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}+2\|ae_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\geq 2a^{2},

so
\max\{d_{+}(\bm{\theta})^{2},d_{-}(\bm{\theta})^{2}\}\geq a^{2}
. Combining with the strong-convexity lower bound gives, for all
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
,

\max\{g_{+}(\bm{\theta})-g_{+}(\bm{\theta}_{+}^{\star}),\;g_{-}(\bm{\theta})-g_{-}(\bm{\theta}_{-}^{\star})\}\;\geq\;\frac{\mu}{8}\max\{d_{+}(\bm{\theta})^{2},d_{-}(\bm{\theta})^{2}\}\;\geq\;\frac{\mu a^{2}}{8}.

Taking
\inf_{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta}
of the left-hand side yields
\chi(g_{+},g_{-})\geq\mu a^{2}/8
. Finally, since
\bm{\theta}_{u}^{\star}=uae_{1}\in\Theta
by the segment assumption, we have

\min_{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta}g_{u}(\bm{\theta})=g_{u}(\bm{\theta}_{u}^{\star})
for each
u
. This proves the second claim.
∎

Next we show that this localization makes
\|\nabla g_{+}-\nabla g_{-}\|_{L^{p}(\Theta)}
of order
\mu a^{1+d/p}
so we can switch between
g_{+}
and
g_{-}
many times while staying within the gradient-variation budget.

Lemma E.5

(Localized gradient difference)
.

Assume
r\asymp a
(i.e.,
c_{-}a\leq r\leq c_{+}a
for universal constants
c_{-},c_{+}>0
).
Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that

\|\nabla g_{+}-\nabla g_{-}\|_{L^{p}(\Theta)}\;\leq\;C\,\mu\,a\,r^{d/p}\;\asymp\;C\,\mu\,a^{1+d/p}.

Equivalently, writing
\alpha:=1+d/p
, we have
\|\nabla g_{+}-\nabla g_{-}\|_{L^{p}(\Theta)}\lesssim\mu a^{\alpha}
.

Proof of
Lemma

E.5
.

From
Lemma

E.3
, we explicitly computed
\nabla_{\theta}g_{u}
. Using this, we find

\nabla g_{+}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla g_{-}(\bm{\theta})=-2\mu a\Big(e_{1}\,\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})+\langle\bm{\theta},e_{1}\rangle\,\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})\Big).

The right-hand side vanishes whenever
\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})=0
, hence it is supported on
B(0,r)
.
On this support,
|\langle\bm{\theta},e_{1}\rangle|\leq\|\bm{\theta}\|_{2}\leq r
, and by the bump derivative bounds

\|\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})\|_{2}\leq C_{\psi}/r
. Therefore, for all
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
,

\|\nabla g_{+}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla g_{-}(\bm{\theta})\|_{2}\leq 2\mu a\Big(\|e_{1}\|_{2}\,|\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})|+|\langle\bm{\theta},e_{1}\rangle|\,\|\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})\|_{2}\Big)\leq 2\mu a\Big(1+r\cdot\frac{C_{\psi}}{r}\Big)\leq C_{0}\,\mu a,

for a universal constant
C_{0}>0
, and the quantity is
0
outside
B(0,r)
. Hence

\|\nabla g_{+}-\nabla g_{-}\|_{L^{p}(\Theta)}=\Big(\int_{\Theta}\|\nabla g_{+}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla g_{-}(\bm{\theta})\|_{2}^{p}\,dx\Big)^{1/p}\leq(C_{0}\mu a)\,|\Theta\cap B(0,r)|^{1/p}\leq(C_{0}\mu a)\,|B(0,r)|^{1/p}.

Using
|B(0,r)|=c_{d}r^{d}
yields

\|\nabla g_{+}-\nabla g_{-}\|_{L^{p}(\Theta)}\leq(C_{0}\mu a)\,(c_{d}r^{d})^{1/p}=C\,\mu\,a\,r^{d/p}.

If additionally
r\asymp a
, then
a\,r^{d/p}\asymp a^{1+d/p}
, giving the claimed scaling.
∎

A simple consequence of
Lemma

E.5
and constructing
g_{u}
through a smooth bump function is that we can obtain pointwise localization as stated in the following result:

Lemma E.6

(Pointwise localization)
.

Let
f_{u}
be defined by (
163
). Then for all
\theta\in\Theta
,

\big\|\nabla g_{+}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla g_{-}(\bm{\theta})\big\|_{2}^{2}\;\leq\;4C\mu^{2}a^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\|\bm{\theta}\|_{2}<r\},

(167)

for some universal
C>0
.

Proof of
Lemma

E.6
.

From
Lemma

E.5
, we found that

\nabla g_{+}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla g_{-}(\bm{\theta})=-2\mu a\Big(e_{1}\,\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})+\langle\bm{\theta},e_{1}\rangle\,\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})\Big).

The right-hand side vanishes whenever
\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})=0
, hence it is supported on
B(0,r)
. On this support,
|\langle\bm{\theta},e_{1}\rangle|\leq\|\bm{\theta}\|_{2}\leq r
, and by the bump derivative bounds
\|\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})\|_{2}\leq C_{\psi}/r
. Using the triangle inequality, we find

\big\|\nabla g_{+}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla g_{-}(\bm{\theta})\big\|_{2}^{2}\leq 4C\mu^{2}a^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\|\bm{\theta}\|_{2}<r\}

which is exactly (
167
) after absorbing constants.
∎

E.3
Inducing sharp momentum penalty into the noise-dominated term

We now show that the noise-dominated (information-theoretic) minimax term inherits sharp dependence on the momentum parameter
\beta
. The key is to exploit that, under noisy gradient feedback, the KL divergence between two environments is proportional to the cumulative squared mean shift observed along the algorithm’s trajectory. For our localized bump construction, this mean shift is supported on a
small region. Define
\mathrm{Occ}(r):=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}\{\|\bm{\theta}_{t}\|_{2}\leq r\}
. We will show that by tuning the bump radius into a rare-visit regime whose visitation probability depends on
\beta
, the expected occupation time
\mathbb{E}^{\pi}[\mathrm{Occ}(r)]
scales with
(1-\beta)
which will in turn cause the KL to scales as if the noise variance were inflated from
\sigma^{2}
to
\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta)
.

Before we prove this claim, recall our localized construction (
163
). Since
\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})=0
and
\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})=0
whenever
\|\bm{\theta}\|_{2}\geq r
, we have the identity
\nabla g_{u}(\bm{\theta})=\mu\,\bm{\theta}

for all
\|\bm{\theta}\|_{2}\geq r
and all
u\in\{+1,-1\}
. Thus, outside the bump region, the (
SGDM
) iterate evolves according to the same linear recursion
(independent of
u
). This is the mechanism we exploit: the visitation frequency of the bump is controlled
by the stationary spread of this linear system, and this spread depends sharply on
\beta
. We will focus on Polyak Heavy-Ball but we note that this analysis can be extended to Nesterov acceleration.

We now quantify the fundamental reason the occupation time depends on momentum: in the presence of gradient noise,
Heavy-Ball (
SGDM
) exhibits a stationary covariance that scales as
(1-\beta)^{-1}
.

Lemma E.7

(Exact stationary variance of Heavy-Ball on a quadratic)
.

Fix
\mu>0
,
\gamma>0
,
\beta\in[0,1)
, and
\sigma>0
. Consider the one-dimensional Heavy-Ball recursion
on the quadratic
x\mapsto\frac{\mu}{2}x^{2}
with additive Gaussian gradient noise:

x_{t+1}=(1+\beta-\gamma\mu)\,x_{t}-\beta\,x_{t-1}-\gamma\,\zeta_{t+1},\qquad\zeta_{t+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}),

(168)

for
t\geq 0
, with some deterministic initialization
(x_{0},x_{-1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Define the state vector
S_{t}:=(x_{t},x_{t-1})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the matrices

A:=\begin{pmatrix}1+\beta-\gamma\mu&-\beta\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad B:=\begin{pmatrix}-\gamma\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

(169)

Then (
168
) is equivalently the linear stochastic system

S_{t+1}=AS_{t}+B\zeta_{t+1}.

(170)

Assume the
stability condition

\rho(A)<1,

(171)

where
\rho(\cdot)
denotes spectral radius. Equivalently, the roots of

\lambda^{2}-(1+\beta-\gamma\mu)\lambda+\beta=0
lie strictly inside the open unit disk. Then:

1.

The Markov chain
(S_{t})_{t\geq 0}
admits a unique stationary distribution
\pi_{\infty}
, which is a centered
Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\infty})
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

2.

The stationary covariance
\Sigma_{\infty}
is the unique positive semidefinite solution of the discrete-time
Lyapunov equation

\Sigma=A\Sigma A^{\top}+\sigma^{2}BB^{\top}.

(172)

3.

Writing
\Sigma_{\infty}=\begin{pmatrix}v&c\\
c&v\end{pmatrix}
with

v=\mathrm{Var}_{\pi_{\infty}}(x_{t})
and
c=\mathrm{Cov}_{\pi_{\infty}}(x_{t},x_{t-1})
, we have the exact closed form

v=\frac{(1+\beta)\gamma^{2}\sigma^{2}}{(1-\beta)\Big((1+\beta)^{2}-(1+\beta-\gamma\mu)^{2}\Big)}=\frac{(1+\beta)\gamma\sigma^{2}}{(1-\beta)\mu\big(2(1+\beta)-\gamma\mu\big)}.

(173)

4.

In the stable regime one necessarily has
\gamma\mu<2(1+\beta)
, so the denominator in (
173
) is positive.
Moreover, if additionally
\gamma\mu\leq 1+\beta
(a common small-step regime), then

v\;\geq\;\frac{\gamma\sigma^{2}}{4\mu(1-\beta)}.

(174)

Proof of
Lemma

E.7
.

We proceed in four steps: (1) existence/uniqueness of a stationary law, (2) characterization via a Lyapunov equation, (3) explicit solution of the
(1,1)
entry,
and (4) extraction of the
(1-\beta)^{-1}
lower bound.

Step 1:
(S_{t})
is an affine Gaussian Markov chain; existence and uniqueness of a stationary law under
\rho(A)<1
.

Fix any deterministic
S_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Iterating (
170
) gives the explicit representation

S_{t}=A^{t}S_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{t}A^{t-k}B\zeta_{k},\qquad t\geq 1.

(175)

Since
(\zeta_{k})_{k\geq 1}
are independent Gaussians and (
175
) is an affine map of the vector

(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{t})
, it follows that each
S_{t}
is Gaussian in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Moreover,
\mathbb{E}[\zeta_{k}]=0
yields

\mathbb{E}[S_{t}]=A^{t}S_{0}.

(176)

Under (
171
), there exists a matrix norm
\|\cdot\|
and constants
C\geq 1
,
\rho\in(0,1)
such that

\|A^{t}\|\leq C\rho^{t}
for all
t\geq 0
(standard consequence of
\rho(A)<1
). Hence
\mathbb{E}[S_{t}]\to 0
as
t\to\infty
. Next compute the covariance. Let
\Sigma_{t}:=\mathrm{Cov}(S_{t})
.
From (
170
), using independence of
\zeta_{t+1}
from
\sigma(S_{0},\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{t})
, we have

\Sigma_{t+1}=A\Sigma_{t}A^{\top}+\sigma^{2}BB^{\top},\qquad t\geq 0,

(177)

with
\Sigma_{0}=0
if
S_{0}
is deterministic. Iterating (
177
) yields the series representation

\Sigma_{t}=\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}A^{j}\,(\sigma^{2}BB^{\top})\,(A^{j})^{\top}\;+\;A^{t}\Sigma_{0}(A^{t})^{\top}.

(178)

The second term vanishes as
t\to\infty
because
\|A^{t}\|\to 0
. The first term converges absolutely in operator norm:
indeed, using
\|A^{j}\|\leq C\rho^{j}
and
\|B\|<\infty
,

\left\|A^{j}\,(\sigma^{2}BB^{\top})\,(A^{j})^{\top}\right\|\leq\sigma^{2}\|A^{j}\|^{2}\|B\|^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}C^{2}\rho^{2j}\|B\|^{2},

and
\sum_{j\geq 0}\rho^{2j}<\infty
. Therefore
\Sigma_{t}\to\Sigma_{\infty}
where

\Sigma_{\infty}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}A^{j}\,(\sigma^{2}BB^{\top})\,(A^{j})^{\top}.

(179)

Define
\pi_{\infty}:=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\infty})
. From (
175
)–(
179
), the law of
S_{t}

converges weakly to
\pi_{\infty}
, and
\pi_{\infty}
is stationary since substituting (
179
) into the covariance recursion yields
(
172
) (verified in Step 2).

For uniqueness, if
\pi
is any stationary distribution with finite second moment, then letting
\Sigma_{\pi}:=\mathrm{Cov}_{\pi}(S)
,
stationarity and (
170
) imply
\Sigma_{\pi}
solves (
172
). Under
\rho(A)<1
, the Lyapunov equation
(
172
) has a unique positive semidefinite solution (see Step 2), hence
\Sigma_{\pi}=\Sigma_{\infty}
; since the chain is Gaussian and linear,
this identifies
\pi
as
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\infty})
. (Equivalently, any stationary law must be Gaussian because the transition kernel is Gaussian and
linear, and the unique second moment determines it.)

Step 2: Lyapunov characterization and uniqueness of the stationary covariance.

We now justify (ii) rigorously. Let
\Sigma
be any solution to (
172
). Vectorize:

\mathrm{vec}(A\Sigma A^{\top})=(A\otimes A)\mathrm{vec}(\Sigma)
, so (
172
) becomes

\big(I-A\otimes A\big)\mathrm{vec}(\Sigma)=\sigma^{2}\mathrm{vec}(BB^{\top}).

(180)

Under
\rho(A)<1
, we have
\rho(A\otimes A)=\rho(A)^{2}<1
, hence
I-A\otimes A
is invertible and
(
180
) admits a unique solution. This proves uniqueness of
\Sigma_{\infty}
. Moreover, the Neumann series yields

\mathrm{vec}(\Sigma_{\infty})=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(A\otimes A)^{j}\sigma^{2}\mathrm{vec}(BB^{\top})=\sigma^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{vec}\!\big(A^{j}BB^{\top}(A^{j})^{\top}\big),

which is exactly (
179
) upon unvectorizing. Hence (
179
) is the unique PSD solution of (
172
).

Step 3: explicit computation of
v=\mathrm{Var}_{\pi_{\infty}}(x_{t})
from (
172
).

Write

\Sigma_{\infty}=\begin{pmatrix}v&c\\
c&w\end{pmatrix},\qquad v=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\infty}}[x_{t}^{2}],\quad c=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\infty}}[x_{t}x_{t-1}],\quad w=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{\infty}}[x_{t-1}^{2}].

Stationarity of
(S_{t})
implies
x_{t}
and
x_{t-1}
have the same marginal law under
\pi_{\infty}
, hence
w=v
.
Thus,

\Sigma_{\infty}=\begin{pmatrix}v&c\\
c&v\end{pmatrix}.

(181)

Next note from (
169
) that
\sigma^{2}BB^{\top}=\sigma^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{2}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
.
Plugging (
181
) into (
172
), we compute
A\Sigma_{\infty}A^{\top}
explicitly.

Let
a:=1+\beta-\gamma\mu
so that
A=\begin{pmatrix}a&-\beta\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
.
First,

A\Sigma_{\infty}=\begin{pmatrix}a&-\beta\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v&c\\
c&v\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}av-\beta c&ac-\beta v\\
v&c\end{pmatrix}.

Then

A\Sigma_{\infty}A^{\top}=\begin{pmatrix}av-\beta c&ac-\beta v\\
v&c\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a&1\\
-\beta&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a^{2}v-2a\beta c+\beta^{2}v&av-\beta c\\
av-\beta c&v\end{pmatrix}.

Now impose (
172
):

\begin{pmatrix}v&c\\
c&v\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a^{2}v-2a\beta c+\beta^{2}v&av-\beta c\\
av-\beta c&v\end{pmatrix}+\sigma^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\gamma^{2}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}.

Equating the
(1,2)
entries gives

c=av-\beta c\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(1+\beta)c=av\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad c=\frac{a}{1+\beta}\,v.

(182)

Equating the
(1,1)
entries gives

v=(a^{2}+\beta^{2})v-2a\beta c+\gamma^{2}\sigma^{2}.

(183)

Substitute (
182
) into (
183
):

v=(a^{2}+\beta^{2})v-2a\beta\left(\frac{a}{1+\beta}v\right)+\gamma^{2}\sigma^{2}=\left(a^{2}+\beta^{2}-\frac{2a^{2}\beta}{1+\beta}\right)v+\gamma^{2}\sigma^{2}.

Rearrange this as:

\left(1-a^{2}-\beta^{2}+\frac{2a^{2}\beta}{1+\beta}\right)v=\gamma^{2}\sigma^{2}.

Solving for
v
yields

v=\frac{(1+\beta)\gamma^{2}\sigma^{2}}{(1-\beta)\big((1+\beta)^{2}-a^{2}\big)}.

Recalling
a=1+\beta-\gamma\mu
gives (
173
), and the alternative expression follows from

(1+\beta)^{2}-(1+\beta-\gamma\mu)^{2}=\gamma\mu\big(2(1+\beta)-\gamma\mu\big).

Step 4: positivity and the
(1-\beta)^{-1}
lower bound.

We first note that the denominator in (
173
) is strictly positive under stability.
Indeed, stability implies in particular that
|a|<1+\beta
for the AR(2) polynomial (equivalently, the roots lie in the unit disk),
hence
(1+\beta)^{2}-a^{2}>0
. More explicitly,

(1+\beta)^{2}-a^{2}=\gamma\mu\big(2(1+\beta)-\gamma\mu\big),

so positivity is equivalent to
\gamma\mu<2(1+\beta)
, which is necessary for stability. Now assume
\gamma\mu\leq 1+\beta
. Then
2(1+\beta)-\gamma\mu\leq 2(1+\beta)
and also
1+\beta\leq 2
.
Using the second form in (
173
),

v=\frac{(1+\beta)\gamma\sigma^{2}}{(1-\beta)\mu\big(2(1+\beta)-\gamma\mu\big)}\;\geq\;\frac{(1+\beta)\gamma\sigma^{2}}{(1-\beta)\mu\cdot 2(1+\beta)}=\frac{\gamma\sigma^{2}}{4\mu(1-\beta)}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Fix
u\in\{+1,-1\}
and let
(\bm{\theta}_{t})
denote Heavy–Ball iterates on
g_{u}
with Gaussian gradient noise
(as in (
SGDM
)). Define the
quadratic-core proxy

(\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{t})
driven
by the
same
noises and the
same
initialization by

\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{t+1}=\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{t}+\beta(\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{t}-\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{t-1})-\gamma(\mu\,\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{t}+\bm{\zeta}_{t+1}),\qquad(\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{0},\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{-1})=(\bm{\theta}_{0},\bm{\theta}_{-1}),

(184)

where
\bm{\zeta}_{t+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{d})
. Since (
184
)
is a linear affine function of
(\bm{\zeta}_{1},\dots,\bm{\zeta}_{t})
, every coordinate of

\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{t}
is Gaussian for each fixed
t
. We use
(\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{t})
to justify a Gaussian small-ball bound. Fix any orthogonal index
j\in\{2,\dots,d\}
and define the projections

x_{t}:=\langle\bm{\theta}_{t}-\bm{\theta}_{u}^{\star},e_{j}\rangle,\qquad\tilde{x}_{t}:=\langle\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{t}-\bm{\theta}_{u}^{\star},e_{j}\rangle,\qquad d_{t}:=x_{t}-\tilde{x}_{t}.

Subtracting the
e_{j}
-coordinates of the true and proxy recursions gives, for all
t\geq 0
,

d_{t+1}=(1+\beta-\gamma\mu)\,d_{t}-\beta\,d_{t-1}+\eta_{t}^{(j)},\qquad\eta_{t}^{(j)}:=\gamma u\cdot\mu a\,\langle\bm{\theta}_{t},e_{1}\rangle\,\partial_{j}\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta}_{t}),

(185)

with
(d_{0},d_{-1})=(0,0)
. Since
\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})=0
for
\|\bm{\theta}\|_{2}\geq r
and

\|\nabla\psi_{r}(\bm{\theta})\|_{2}\leq C_{\psi}/r
, we have on the support of
\eta_{t}^{(j)}
that

|\langle\bm{\theta}_{t},e_{1}\rangle|\leq\|\bm{\theta}_{t}\|_{2}\leq r
. This results in

|\eta_{t}^{(j)}|\leq C_{\psi}\gamma\mu a\,\qquad\forall t\geq 0.

(186)

Let
A=\begin{pmatrix}1+\beta-\gamma\mu&-\beta\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
be the companion matrix from
(
169
). In the small-step regime

0<\gamma\mu\leq\frac{1-\beta}{4},

(187)

the eigenvalues of
A
are real and lie in
(0,1)
, and a standard diagonalization argument yields

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|A^{k}\|_{2}\leq C\,(1-\beta)/(\gamma\mu)
for a universal constant
C>0
.
Iterating (
185
) from
(d_{0},d_{-1})=(0,0)
and using (
186
) therefore gives the
uniform bound

\sup_{t\geq 0}|d_{t}|\leq\Delta,\qquad\Delta:=C_{\Delta}\,a(1+C_{\psi})(1-\beta),

(188)

for a universal constant
C_{\Delta}>0
(absorbing numerical factors into
C_{\Delta}
). Finally, for every
t
we have deterministically

\{\|\bm{\theta}_{t}-\bm{\theta}_{u}^{\star}\|_{2}\leq r\}\subseteq\{|x_{t}|\leq r\}\subseteq\{|\tilde{x}_{t}|\leq r+\Delta\},

where the first inclusion uses
|\langle v,e_{j}\rangle|\leq\|v\|_{2}
and the second uses
|\tilde{x}_{t}|\leq|x_{t}|+|d_{t}|
with
(
188
). Consequently,

\mathrm{Occ}_{T}(r):=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}\{\|\bm{\theta}_{t}-\bm{\theta}_{u}^{\star}\|_{2}\leq r\}\;\leq\;\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}\{|\tilde{x}_{t}|\leq r+\Delta\}.

(189)

Thus it suffices to bound Gaussian small-ball probabilities for the proxy coordinate
\tilde{x}_{t}
.

Lemma E.8

(
\beta
-dependent occupation bound in the rare-visit regime)
.

Assume
\rho(A)<1
and
\gamma\mu\leq 1+\beta
so that Lemma
E.7
applies.
Let
\tilde{x}_{t}
be the proxy coordinate defined above, and write
v_{\infty}:=\mathrm{Var}_{\pi_{\infty}}(\tilde{x})

for the stationary variance of the one-dimensional quadratic-core recursion (so
v_{\infty}
satisfies (
174
)).
Then for every
R>0
,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}\{|\tilde{x}_{t}|\leq R\}\right]\;\leq\;T\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\;\frac{R}{\sqrt{v_{\infty}}}\;+\;T_{0},

(190)

where
T_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
is a finite burn-in index (depending only on
(\beta,\gamma\mu)
and the initialization)
such that
\mathrm{Var}(\tilde{x}_{t})\geq v_{\infty}/2
for all
t\geq T_{0}
.
In particular, combining (
189
) with (
190
) at
R=r+\Delta
yields

\mathbb{E}^{u}[\mathrm{Occ}_{T}(r)]\lesssim C_{1}\,T\cdot(r+\Delta)\sqrt{\frac{\mu(1-\beta)}{\gamma\sigma^{2}}}

(191)

for a universal constant
C_{1}>0
. Consequently, if we choose the bump radius in the rare-visit regime

r\;:=\;c_{r}\;\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\gamma(1-\beta)}{\mu}}

(192)

and simultaneously calibrate the bump amplitude
\alpha
so that
\Delta\leq r
(equivalently,

C_{A}C_{\psi}\gamma\mu\alpha\leq r
), then

\mathbb{E}^{u}[\mathrm{Occ}_{T}(r)]\lesssim C_{2}\,T(1-\beta)

(193)

for a universal constant
C_{2}>0
.

Proof of
Lemma

E.8
.

Fix
R>0
. For any Gaussian random variable
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(m,v)
and any
R>0
, we have the uniform small-ball bound

\mathbb{P}(|Z|\leq R)=\int_{-R}^{R}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi v}}\exp\!\left(-\frac{(z-m)^{2}}{2v}\right)\,dz\leq\int_{-R}^{R}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi v}}\,dz=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{R}{\sqrt{v}}.

Since
\tilde{x}_{t}
is Gaussian for each fixed
t
, this implies

\mathbb{P}(|\tilde{x}_{t}|\leq R)\leq\sqrt{2/\pi}\,R/\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(\tilde{x}_{t})}
for all
t
. Since the covariance of the stable linear system (
184
) converges to its unique stationary covariance (
Lemma

E.7
), there exists a finite
T_{0}
such that
\mathrm{Var}(\tilde{x}_{t})\geq v_{\infty}/2
for all
t\geq T_{0}
.
Therefore,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbf{1}\{|\tilde{x}_{t}|\leq R\}\right]=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{P}(|\tilde{x}_{t}|\leq R)\leq T_{0}+\sum_{t=T_{0}}^{T-1}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{R}{\sqrt{v_{\infty}/2}}\leq T_{0}+T\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{R}{\sqrt{v_{\infty}}},

which is (
190
). Now take
R=r+\Delta
and combine with the deterministic reduction (
189
) to obtain

\mathbb{E}^{u}[\mathrm{Occ}_{T}(r)]\leq T\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{r+\Delta}{\sqrt{v_{\infty}}}+T_{0}.

Finally, use Lemma
E.7
, in particular (
174
), to bound

1/\sqrt{v_{\infty}}\leq 2\sqrt{\mu(1-\beta)/(\gamma\sigma^{2})}
, yielding (
191
).
Substituting the rare-visit choice (
192
) and enforcing
\Delta\leq r
gives

r+\Delta\leq 2r=2c_{r}\sigma\sqrt{\gamma(1-\beta)/\mu}
, which plugged into (
191
) yields
(
193
) after absorbing numerical factors into
C_{2}
.
∎

E.4
Constructing a
J
-block hard family

We now build a
J
-block packing family of nonstationary sequences
G^{u}_{1:T}
indexed by sign vectors

u\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Delta_{T}}
, where each block uses one of the two base losses
\{g_{+},g_{-}\}
. Fix an integer
1\leq J\leq T
(to be tuned later) and set
\Delta_{T}:=\lfloor T/J\rfloor
. Partition the horizon

[T]:=\{1,\dots,T\}
into
J
disjoint consecutive batches (blocks)
B_{1},\dots,B_{J}
such that each
B_{j}
has
cardinality either
\Delta_{T}
or
\Delta_{T}+1
and
\bigcup_{j=1}^{J}B_{j}=[T]
. For each block
j\in[J]
, let

|B_{j}|
denote its length and write its indices as

B_{j}=\{t_{j}(1),t_{j}(2),\dots,t_{j}(|B_{j}|)\},\qquad t_{j}(1)<\cdots<t_{j}(|B_{j}|).

Let
\{\pm 1\}^{J}
be the class of sign vectors of length
J
, and let
\mathcal{U}\subset\{\pm 1\}^{J}
. For any
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{J})\in\mathcal{U}
, define the nonstationary
loss sequence
G^{u}_{1:T}
by holding the loss fixed within each block,

G_{t}^{u}(\cdot)\;:=\;g_{u_{j}}(\cdot)\qquad\text{for all }t\in B_{j},\;j\in[J].

(194)

Thus
G^{u}
can change only at block boundaries while encoding one bit per block. To apply Fano’s method we require a large subset
\mathcal{U}\subset\{\pm 1\}^{J}
whose elements are well separated in Hamming distance. We obtain such a set via a constant-weight code construction. This is the same construction used by
(
7
)
and originates from
(
22
)
and
(
64
)
gave an explicit lower bound which we cite below. For simplicity assume
J
is even (the odd case follows by restricting to

J-1
coordinates).

Lemma E.9

(
(
64
)
, Lemma 9)
.

Suppose
J\geq 2
is even. There exists a subset
\mathcal{I}\subset\{0,1\}^{J}
such that
(i) every
i\in\mathcal{I}
has exactly
J/2
ones, i.e.
\sum_{j=1}^{J}i_{j}=J/2
; and
(ii) every pair
i\neq i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
satisfies
\Delta_{H}(i,i^{\prime})\geq J/16
, where
\Delta_{H}
is Hamming distance.
Moreover,
\log|\mathcal{I}|\geq 0.0625\,J
.

By
Lemma

E.9
, any two indices
u\neq v
disagree on at least
J/8
blocks; equivalently, the sequences
G^{u}
and
G^{v}
differ on at least
\sum_{j:\,u_{j}\neq v_{j}}|B_{j}|\geq(J/8)\Delta_{T}\gtrsim T
time indices,
which is precisely the separation needed to convert a constant testing error (via Fano) into an
\Omega(T)
regret gap. We next bound the gradient-variation functional
\mathrm{GVar}_{p,q}
for the
J
-block construction.

Lemma E.10

(
\mathrm{GVar}_{p,q}
bound for the
J
-block construction)
.

Let
1\leq J\leq T
and set
\Delta_{T}:=\lfloor T/J\rfloor
. Consider the
J
disjoint consecutive blocks

B_{1},\dots,B_{J}
with
|B_{j}|\in\{\Delta_{T},\Delta_{T}+1\}
and the blockwise-constant sequence

G^{u}_{1:T}
defined by (
194
) for some
u\in\{\pm 1\}^{J}
. Then, for every such
u
,

\mathrm{GVar}_{p,q}(G^{u}_{1:T})\;\leq\;C\,\mu\,a^{\alpha}\,J^{1/q},\qquad\alpha:=1+\frac{d}{p},

(195)

where
C>0
is a universal constant (depending only on the bump construction in
\psi_{r}
through constants
such as
C_{\psi}
in
Lemma

E.5
).

Proof of
Lemma

E.10
.

By construction,
G_{t}^{u}
is constant on each block
B_{j}
, hence the gradient can change only at a boundary
between consecutive blocks. More precisely, if
t
is a boundary time with
t\in B_{j}
and
t+1\in B_{j+1}
,
then by (
194
),

\nabla G_{t+1}^{u}-\nabla G_{t}^{u}\;=\;\nabla g_{u_{j+1}}-\nabla g_{u_{j}}.

If
u_{j+1}=u_{j}
, the right-hand side is
0
. If
u_{j+1}\neq u_{j}
, then

\nabla g_{u_{j+1}}-\nabla g_{u_{j}}=\pm(\nabla g_{+}-\nabla g_{-})
and therefore, by

Lemma

E.5
,

\big\|\nabla G_{t+1}^{u}-\nabla G_{t}^{u}\big\|_{L^{p}(\Theta)}\;\leq\;\|\nabla g_{+}-\nabla g_{-}\|_{L^{p}(\Theta)}\;\leq\;C\,\mu\,a^{\alpha}.

There are at most
J-1
such boundaries, and all other times contribute zero. Hence, for
1\leq q<\infty
,

\mathrm{GVar}_{p,q}(G^{u}_{1:T})=\Bigg(\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}\big\|\nabla G_{t+1}^{u}-\nabla G_{t}^{u}\big\|_{L^{p}(\Theta)}^{\,q}\Bigg)^{\!1/q}\;\leq\;\Big((J-1)\,(C\mu a^{\alpha})^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\;\leq\;C\,\mu\,a^{\alpha}\,J^{1/q},

after adjusting
C
by a universal factor. The case
q=\infty
is analogous and yields

\mathrm{GVar}_{p,\infty}(G^{u}_{1:T})\leq C\,\mu\,a^{\alpha}
since the maximum increment occurs at a boundary.
∎

We now show that if the algorithm cannot identity
u
, it must incur regret
\gtrsim\mu a^{2}
. This proof is rather elementary and follows simply from the definition of the discrepancy measure and
Lemma

E.4
. We include it for completeness.

Lemma E.11

(Per-round separation)
.

Let
g_{+},g_{-}:\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
be the two base losses, with minimizers

\bm{\theta}_{+}^{\star}\in\arg\min_{\Theta}g_{+}
and
\bm{\theta}_{-}^{\star}\in\arg\min_{\Theta}g_{-}
.
Define the discrepancy

\chi(g_{+},g_{-})\;:=\;\inf_{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta}\max\Big\{g_{+}(\bm{\theta})-g_{+}(\bm{\theta}_{+}^{\star}),\;g_{-}(\bm{\theta})-g_{-}(\bm{\theta}_{-}^{\star})\Big\}.

Then for every
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
,

\big(g_{+}(\bm{\theta})-g_{+}(\bm{\theta}_{+}^{\star})\big)+\big(g_{-}(\bm{\theta})-g_{-}(\bm{\theta}_{-}^{\star})\big)\;\geq\;\chi(g_{+},g_{-}).

(196)

In particular, under the conditions of
Lemma

E.4
, we have

\big(g_{+}(\bm{\theta})-g_{+}(\bm{\theta}_{+}^{\star})\big)+\big(g_{-}(\bm{\theta})-g_{-}(\bm{\theta}_{-}^{\star})\big)\;\geq\;\chi(g_{+},g_{-})\;\geq\;\frac{\mu a^{2}}{8}\qquad\forall\,\bm{\theta}\in\Theta.

(197)

Proof of
Lemma

E.11
.

Fix any
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
. By the definition of
\chi(g_{+},g_{-})
,

\max\Big\{g_{+}(\bm{\theta})-g_{+}(\bm{\theta}_{+}^{\star}),\;g_{-}(\bm{\theta})-g_{-}(\bm{\theta}_{-}^{\star})\Big\}\;\geq\;\chi(g_{+},g_{-}).

Using the elementary inequality
A+B\geq\max\{A,B\}
for all real numbers
A,B
, we obtain (
196
).
The strengthened bound (
197
) follows immediately by combining (
196
) with

Lemma

E.4
, which gives
\chi(g_{+},g_{-})\geq\mu a^{2}/8
.
∎

This immediately gives us a lower bound on the quantity
\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}g,\tilde{g}\in\Theta\end{subarray}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\chi(g_{t},\tilde{g}_{t})

Corollary E.1

(Blockwise accumulation of discrepancy)
.

Fix
1\leq J\leq T
and set
\Delta_{T}:=\lfloor T/J\rfloor
. Let
B_{1},\dots,B_{J}
be a partition of
[T]
into
consecutive blocks with
|B_{j}|\in\{\Delta_{T},\Delta_{T}+1\}
. For any
u,v\in\{\pm 1\}^{J}
, define the
blockwise-constant sequences
G^{u}_{1:T}
and
G^{v}_{1:T}
via (
194
), i.e.,

G_{t}^{u}\equiv g_{u_{j}}\quad\text{and}\quad G_{t}^{v}\equiv g_{v_{j}}\qquad\text{for all }t\in B_{j},\ j\in[J].

Assume the per-round separation bound holds:

\chi(g_{+},g_{-})\;\geq\;\frac{\mu a^{2}}{8}.

(198)

Then for any
u,v\in\{\pm 1\}^{J}
,

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\chi(G_{t}^{u},G_{t}^{v})\;\geq\;\frac{\mu a^{2}}{8}\sum_{j=1}^{J}|B_{j}|\,\mathbf{1}\{u_{j}\neq v_{j}\}\;\geq\;\frac{\mu a^{2}}{8}\,\Delta_{T}\,d_{H}(u,v),

(199)

where
d_{H}(u,v):=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{1}\{u_{j}\neq v_{j}\}
is the Hamming distance. In particular, if

\mathcal{U}\subset\{\pm 1\}^{J}
satisfies
d_{H}(u,v)\geq J/8
for all distinct
u,v\in\mathcal{U}
, then for all

u\neq v
in
\mathcal{U}
,

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\chi(G_{t}^{u},G_{t}^{v})\;\geq\;c_{0}\,\mu a^{2}\,T,

(200)

for a universal constant
c_{0}>0
.

Proof of
Corollary

E.1
.

Fix two distinct codewords
u,v\in\mathcal{U}
and recall that the horizon is partitioned into disjoint
blocks
B_{1},\dots,B_{J}
, each of size
|B_{j}|\in\{\Delta_{T},\Delta_{T}+1\}
with
\Delta_{T}:=\lfloor T/J\rfloor
. Fix an arbitrary
block
B_{j}
and (w.l.o.g.) assume
|B_{j}|=\Delta_{T}
; the blocks of length
\Delta_{T}+1
can be absorbed into constants
since
T
and
J
are large.

If
u_{j}=v_{j}
, then
G_{t}^{u}\equiv G_{t}^{v}
throughout
B_{j}
, hence
\chi(G_{t}^{u},G_{t}^{v})=0
for all
t\in B_{j}
. If instead
u_{j}\neq v_{j}
, then along the entire block we have
(G_{t}^{u},G_{t}^{v})\in\{(g_{+},g_{-}),(g_{-},g_{+})\}
, and therefore by
Lemma

E.11
,

\chi(G_{t}^{u},G_{t}^{v})\;=\;\chi(g_{u_{j}},g_{v_{j}})\;\geq\;\chi(g_{+},g_{-})\;\geq\;\frac{\mu a^{2}}{8}\qquad\text{for all }t\in B_{j}.

Summing over the block yields the blockwise contribution

\sum_{t\in B_{j}}\chi(G_{t}^{u},G_{t}^{v})\;\geq\;m\cdot\frac{\mu a^{2}}{8}\qquad\text{whenever }u_{j}\neq v_{j}.

Consequently, summing over all mismatched blocks gives

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\chi(G_{t}^{u},G_{t}^{v})\;=\;\sum_{j:\,u_{j}\neq v_{j}}\ \sum_{t\in B_{j}}\chi(G_{t}^{u},G_{t}^{v})\;\geq\;m\cdot\Delta_{H}(u,v)\cdot\frac{\mu a^{2}}{8},

where
\Delta_{H}(u,v):=\big|\{j\in[J]:u_{j}\neq v_{j}\}\big|
is the Hamming distance.
Finally, by the Varshamov–Gilbert packing property of
\mathcal{U}
(
Lemma

E.9
), any distinct
u\neq v
satisfy

d_{H}(u,v)\geq K/8
, and since
m=\lfloor T/K\rfloor
we obtain

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\chi(G_{t}^{u},G_{t}^{v})\;\geq\;\frac{J}{8}\cdot\Big\lfloor\frac{T}{J}\Big\rfloor\cdot\frac{\mu a^{2}}{8}\;\geq\;c_{0}\,\mu a^{2}\,T,

for a universal constant
c_{0}>0
(absorbing the floor effect into constants). In words, two sequences that disagree on a constant fraction of blocks incur a constant per-round discrepancy on each such block, and this discrepancy accumulates over
\Theta(T)
rounds.
∎

It remains to upper bound
\sup_{g,\widetilde{g}\in\Theta}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{g}\,\|\,P_{\widetilde{g}})
. Using the noisy gradient feedback model, this follows simply from a Gaussian KL chain rule.

Lemma E.12

(KL control under Gaussian gradient noise)
.

Fix any admissible policy
\pi
and two environments
u,v\in\mathcal{U}
. Let

P_{u}^{\pi}
and
P_{v}^{\pi}
denote the joint laws of

Z_{1:T}:=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{T})
generated under environments
u
and
v
, respectively, when the learner follows
\pi
.
Assume the (conditional) Gaussian gradient feedback model

Y_{t}\;=\;\nabla G_{t}(\bm{\theta}_{t-1})+\varepsilon_{t},\qquad\varepsilon_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{d}),

where
\bm{\theta}_{t-1}
is
\sigma(Y_{1:t-1})
–measurable under
\pi
.
Then the KL divergence satisfies the chain-rule identity

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{u}^{\pi}\,\|\,P_{v}^{\pi})\;=\;\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{u}\!\left[\big\|\nabla G_{t}^{u}(\bm{\theta}_{t-1})-\nabla G_{t}^{v}(\bm{\theta}_{t-1})\big\|_{2}^{2}\right].

(201)

Moreover, for the block construction with base losses
\{g_{+},g_{-}\}
, we have the uniform bound

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{u}^{\pi}\,\|\,P_{v}^{\pi})\;\leq\;C\,\frac{\mu^{2}a^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\,T,

(202)

for a universal constant
C>0
(using
\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\Theta}\|\nabla g_{+}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla g_{-}(\bm{\theta})\|_{2}\leq C^{\prime}\mu a
from
Lemma

E.5
).

Proof of
Lemma

E.12
.

We compute
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{u}^{\pi}\|P_{v}^{\pi})
by conditioning on the learner’s past.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}:=\sigma(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{t-1})
denote the natural filtration.
By admissibility of
\pi
, the iterate
\bm{\theta}_{t-1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
–measurable, and under environment
w\in\{u,v\}
,
the conditional law of
Y_{t}
given
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
is Gaussian:

Y_{t}\,\big|\,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\;\sim\;\mathcal{N}\big(\nabla G_{t}^{w}(\bm{\theta}_{t-1}),\,\sigma^{2}I_{d}\big).

Using the chain rule for KL divergence,

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{u}^{\pi}\|P_{v}^{\pi})=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{u}\!\left[D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left(P_{u}^{\pi}(Y_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1})\,\big\|\,P_{v}^{\pi}(Y_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1})\right)\right].

(203)

Since the two conditional distributions in (
203
) are Gaussians with the same covariance
\sigma^{2}I_{d}
,
their conditional KL is

D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left(\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\sigma^{2}I_{d})\,\big\|\,\mathcal{N}(m_{2},\sigma^{2}I_{d})\right)=\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\|m_{1}-m_{2}\|_{2}^{2}.

Substituting
m_{1}=\nabla G_{t}^{u}(\bm{\theta}_{t-1})
and
m_{2}=\nabla G_{t}^{v}(\bm{\theta}_{t-1})
into (
203
)
yields (
201
). By
Lemma

E.6
, we have that
\big\|\nabla g_{+}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla g_{-}(\bm{\theta})\big\|_{2}^{2}\;\leq\;4C\mu^{2}a^{2}\mathbf{1}\{\|\bm{\theta}\|_{2}<r\}
. This results in

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{u}^{\pi}\,\|\,P_{v}^{\pi})\leq\frac{2C\mu^{2}a^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{u}[\mathbf{1}\{\|\bm{\theta}_{t-1}\|_{2}<r\}]=\frac{2C\mu^{2}a^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{u}[\mathrm{Occ}_{T}(r)].

Combining this with
Lemma

E.8
, we conclude that

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{u}^{\pi}\,\|\,P_{v}^{\pi})\leq\frac{2C^{\prime}\mu^{2}a^{2}}{\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta)}T.

∎

E.5
Obtaining a minimax lower bound in the statistical/variation-limited regime

We can get a bound on
\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\ \sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\ \mathbb{P}_{\theta}\!\left[\widehat{\theta}\neq\theta\right]
via Fano’s inequality:

Corollary E.2

(Fano lower bound via KL control)
.

Fix any admissible policy
\pi
and let
\mathcal{U}\subset\{\pm 1\}^{J}
be a packing set with

\log|\mathcal{U}|\ \geq\ \frac{J}{8}\log 2.

(204)

Let
U
be uniformly distributed on
\mathcal{U}
, and let
Z_{1:T}:=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{T})
denote the transcript
generated under environment
U
when the learner follows policy
\pi
; write
P_{u}^{\pi}
for the law of

Z_{1:T}
under environment
u\in\mathcal{U}
. Then for any estimator
\widehat{U}=\widehat{U}(Z_{1:T})
,

\mathbb{P}(\widehat{U}\neq U)\;\geq\;1-\frac{I(U;Z_{1:T})+\log 2}{\log|\mathcal{U}|}.

(205)

In particular, if the Gaussian-gradient KL bound of
Lemma

E.12
holds so that

\sup_{u,v\in\mathcal{U}}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{u}^{\pi}\,\|\,P_{v}^{\pi})\;\leq\;C\,\frac{\mu^{2}a^{2}}{\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta)}\,T,

(206)

then
I(U;Z_{1:T})\leq C\,\mu^{2}a^{2}T/(\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta))
. Consequently, if

C\,\frac{\mu^{2}a^{2}}{\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta)}\,T\;\leq\;\frac{1}{16}\,J\log 2,

(207)

then every estimator
\widehat{U}
obeys the constant error lower bound

\mathbb{P}(\widehat{U}\neq U)\ \geq\ \frac{1}{2}.

(208)

Proof of
Corollary

E.2
.

Combining
Lemma

G.7
with (
206
) yields

I(U;Z_{1:T})\;\leq\;\frac{1}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\sum_{u,v\in\mathcal{U}}C\,\frac{\mu^{2}a^{2}}{\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta)}\,T\;=\;C\,\frac{\mu^{2}a^{2}}{\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta)}\,T.

Substituting this into (
205
) and using (
204
) gives

\mathbb{P}(\widehat{U}\neq U)\;\geq\;1-\frac{C\,\mu^{2}a^{2}T/(\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta))+\log 2}{(J/8)\log 2}.

Under the condition (
207
), the numerator is at most
(J/16)\log 2+\log 2
, hence for all

J\geq 16
the right-hand side is at least
1/2
after adjusting constants (and for bounded
J
this only
changes constants in the final lower bound). This yields (
208
).
∎

We can finally conclude with
Lemma

E.1
and the upper and lower bounds we have on the KL divergence and discrepancy measure
\chi
respectively.

Lemma E.13

(Parameter tuning for the information-limited lower bound)
.

Fix
1\leq p\leq\infty
,
1\leq q\leq\infty
, and set
\alpha:=1+d/p
. Consider the
J
-block construction

\{G^{u}_{1:T}:u\in\mathcal{U}\}
with block length
\Delta_{T}:=\lfloor T/J\rfloor
and base losses
\{g_{+},g_{-}\}
.
Assume:

1.

Gradient-variation bound:
from
Lemma

E.10
, we have
\mathrm{GVar}_{p,q}(G^{u}_{1:T})\ \leq\ C\,\mu\,a^{\alpha}\,J^{1/q},\;\;\;\forall u\in\mathcal{U}
;

2.

Gaussian-gradient KL bound:
from
Lemma

E.12
, we have
\sup_{u,v\in\mathcal{U}}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{u}^{\pi}\,\|\,P_{v}^{\pi})\ \leq\ C\mu^{2}a^{2}T/(\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta)),\;\;\;\forall\pi
;

3.

Packing size lower bound:
from
Lemma

E.9
, we have the packing size lower bound
\log|\mathcal{U}|\geq c\,J
for a universal constant
c>0
(e.g., from Varshamov–Gilbert or constant-weight codes).

Let
\mathbb{V}_{T}>0
be the variation budget. Choose

a\ :=\ \Big(\frac{\mathbb{V}_{T}}{C\,\mu\,J^{1/q}}\Big)^{\!1/\alpha}.

(209)

Then
\mathrm{GVar}_{p,q}(G^{u}_{1:T})\leq\mathbb{V}_{T}
for all
u\in\mathcal{U}
. Moreover, if
J
additionally satisfies

C\,\frac{\mu^{2}a^{2}}{\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta)}\,T\ \leq\ \frac{c}{2}\,J,

(210)

then Fano’s method yields a constant testing error (hence a constant separation in regret) for the family

\{G^{u}_{1:T}:u\in\mathcal{U}\}
under any policy. In particular, substituting (
209
) into (
210
) shows it suffices to take

J\ \gtrsim\ \left(\frac{\mu^{2-2/\alpha}}{\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta)}\,\mathbb{V}_{T}^{2/\alpha}\,T\right)^{\alpha q/(\alpha q+2)},

(211)

and for this choice one obtains the scaling

\displaystyle\inf_{\pi\in\Pi_{\beta}}\sup_{G:\,\mathrm{GVar}_{p,q}(G)\leq\mathbb{V}_{T}}\mathcal{R}_{T}^{\pi}(G)\;\gtrsim\;(1-\beta)^{-2/(\alpha q+2)}\sigma^{4/(\alpha q+2)}\mu^{(\alpha q-2q-2)/(\alpha q+2)}\mathbb{V}_{T}^{2q/(\alpha q+2)}T^{\alpha q/(\alpha q+2)},

(212)

where
\alpha=1+d/p
.

Proof of
Lemma

E.13
.

First note that by
Corollary

E.2
, we have that
\mathbb{P}(\widehat{U}\neq U)\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Subsequently invoking
Lemma

E.1
, we conclude that there does not exist an admissible policy
\pi\in\Pi_{\beta}
such that
\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{p,q}(\mathbb{V}_{T})}\mathcal{R}_{T}^{\pi,\phi^{G}}(g)\;\leq\;\frac{1}{9}\,\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}g,\tilde{g}\in\Theta\end{subarray}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\chi(g_{t},\tilde{g}_{t})
. This gives us a lower bound for the minimax regret. Now we must just enforce the gradient variational budget and the information constraint. To enforce the gradient variational budget, we have by
Lemma

E.10
that the condition
\mathrm{GVar}_{p,q}(G^{u}_{1:T})\leq\mathbb{V}_{T}
for all
u\in\mathcal{U}
is ensured
whenever
C\mu a^{\alpha}J^{1/q}\leq\mathbb{V}_{T}
, which is exactly achieved by the choice (
209
).

To enforce the information (Fano/KL) constraint,, we have by
Lemma

E.12
, for any policy
\pi
and any
u,v\in\mathcal{U}
,

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{u}^{\pi}\,\|\,P_{v}^{\pi})\ \leq\ C\,\frac{\mu^{2}a^{2}}{\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta)}\,T.

Combining this uniform pairwise bound with the standard mutual-information upper bound (
Lemma

G.7
), we get

I(U;Z_{1:T})\ \leq\ C\,\frac{\mu^{2}a^{2}}{\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta)}\,T.

Since
\log|\mathcal{U}|\geq cJ
, imposing (
210
) makes
I(U;Z_{1:T})
a small constant fraction of

\log|\mathcal{U}|
. Fano’s inequality then yields a constant lower bound on the minimax probability of misidentifying
U
, uniformly over all estimators
\widehat{U}(Z_{1:T})
.

Substituting (
209
) into (
210
) gives

C\,\frac{\mu^{2}T}{\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta)}\left(\frac{\mathbb{V}_{T}}{C\mu J^{1/q}}\right)^{\!2/\alpha}\ \lesssim\ J,

or equivalently,

\frac{\mu^{2-2/\alpha}}{\sigma^{2}/(1-\beta)}\,\mathbb{V}_{T}^{2/\alpha}\,T\ \lesssim\ J^{\,1+\frac{2}{\alpha q}}.

Rearranging yields (
211
). Finally, the quantity that drives the regret separation in the information-limited regime is
\mu a^{2}T
. Using (
209
), plugging
J\asymp J_{\star}
from (
211
), and simplifying exponents yields we get

\inf_{\pi\in\Pi_{\beta}}\sup_{G:\,\mathrm{GVar}_{p,q}(G)\leq\mathbb{V}_{T}}\mathcal{R}_{T}^{\pi}(G)\;\gtrsim\;(1-\beta)^{-2/(\alpha q+2)}\sigma^{4/(\alpha q+2)}\mu^{(\alpha q-2q-2)/(\alpha q+2)}\mathbb{V}_{T}^{2q/(\alpha q+2)}T^{\alpha q/(\alpha q+2)}.

∎

E.6
Obtaining a minimax lower bound in the inertia-limited regime

Our information-theoretic construction (packing + Fano/KL) yields a lower bound that is driven by

statistical indistinguishability
of environments and therefore captures the dependence on the noise level

\sigma
and the variation budget
\mathbb{V}_{T}
. However, it does not by itself explain the empirically dominant
failure mode of momentum under drift:
inertia
. To isolate this mechanism, we analyze (
SGDM
) (Heavy-Ball)
on the simplest strongly convex and smooth objective,

\phi_{u}(z)=\frac{\mu}{2}(z-ua)^{2}
, where the minimizer jumps between
\pm a
. We prove the momentum-specific lower bound by explicitly analyzing (
SGDM
) on a single block switch and showing it takes
\tau_{\beta}:=\Omega(\kappa/(1-\beta))
steps to reduce the tracking error by a constant factor under a step size restriction. Crucially, this part does not rely on hiding information; the learner may instantly know the new function. The lower bound comes from the algorithmic constraint of (
SGDM
) under stability. We will focus on the Polyak Heavy-Ball method of momentum (
\beta_{1}=0,\beta_{2}=\beta
). However a similar analysis can be carried out for Nesterov and yield a analogous result.

Proposition E.1

((
SGDM
) on a 1D quadratic)
.

Fix
u\in\{\pm 1\}
and consider the one-dimensional
\mu
–strongly
\mu
-smooth convex quadratic
\phi_{u}(z)\;:=\;\frac{\mu}{2}(z-ua)^{2},\;\;z\in\mathbb{R}
. Let Heavy-Ball (
SGDM
) (Polyak momentum) with step size
\gamma>0
and momentum
\beta\in[0,1)
evolve as

z_{t+1}=z_{t}+\beta(z_{t}-z_{t-1})-\gamma\big(\mu(z_{t}-ua)+\eta_{t}\big),\qquad\eta_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})\ \text{i.i.d.}

(213)

Define the error
e_{t}:=z_{t}-ua
. Then the mean error satisfies the deterministic linear recursion

\binom{\mathbb{E}[e_{t+1}]}{\mathbb{E}[e_{t}]}\;=\;\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}1+\beta-\gamma\mu&-\beta\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\;A}\binom{\mathbb{E}[e_{t}]}{\mathbb{E}[e_{t-1}]},

(214)

and hence
\|\binom{\mathbb{E}[e_{t+1}]}{\mathbb{E}[e_{t}]}\|_{2}\leq\|A\|_{2}^{\,t}\|\binom{\mathbb{E}[e_{1}]}{\mathbb{E}[e_{0}]}\|_{2}
.
Moreover, let
\lambda_{\max}
denote the eigenvalue of
A
with maximal modulus. If

0<\gamma\mu\leq\min\Big\{(1-\sqrt{\beta})^{2},\ \frac{1-\beta}{4}\Big\},

(215)

then
A
has two real eigenvalues in
(0,1)
and there exists a universal constant
c>0
such that

|\lambda_{\max}|\;\geq\;1-c\,\frac{\gamma\mu}{1-\beta}.

(216)

Consequently, for any initialization with
\mathbb{E}[e_{0}]=\mathbb{E}[e_{-1}]=a
, there exists a universal constant
C\geq 1

such that for all
t\geq 0
,

|\mathbb{E}[e_{t}]|\;\geq\;C^{-1}|\lambda_{\max}|^{\,t}\,a\;\geq\;C^{-1}\Big(1-c\,\frac{\gamma\mu}{1-\beta}\Big)^{t}a.

(217)

In particular, defining the response time

\tau_{\beta}\;:=\;\min\Big\{t\geq 0:\ \Big(1-c\,\frac{\gamma\mu}{1-\beta}\Big)^{t}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Big\},

(218)

we have the scaling

\tau_{\beta}\ \asymp\ \frac{1-\beta}{\gamma\mu}.

(219)

Finally, Jensen’s inequality yields the expected suboptimality lower bound

\mathbb{E}\big[\phi_{u}(z_{t})-\phi_{u}(ua)\big]=\frac{\mu}{2}\mathbb{E}[e_{t}^{2}]\;\geq\;\frac{\mu}{2}\big(\mathbb{E}[e_{t}]\big)^{2}\;\gtrsim\;\mu a^{2}\Big(1-c\,\frac{\gamma\mu}{1-\beta}\Big)^{2t}.

(220)

Proof of
Proposition

E.1
.

Subtracting
ua
from (
213
) gives

e_{t+1}=(1+\beta-\gamma\mu)e_{t}-\beta e_{t-1}-\gamma\eta_{t}.

(221)

Taking expectations and using
\mathbb{E}[\eta_{t}]=0
yields the homogeneous recursion

\mathbb{E}[e_{t+1}]=(1+\beta-\gamma\mu)\mathbb{E}[e_{t}]-\beta\mathbb{E}[e_{t-1}],

which is equivalently the two-dimensional linear system (
214
) with matrix
A
as stated. The eigenvalues of
A
are the roots of

\lambda^{2}-(1+\beta-\gamma\mu)\lambda+\beta=0.

(222)

Under (
215
), the discriminant is nonnegative so
\lambda_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,
\lambda_{\pm}\in(0,1)
in this regime. Let
\lambda_{\max}:=\max\{\lambda_{+},\lambda_{-}\}
. To lower bound
\lambda_{\max}
, write
\delta:=1-\beta\in(0,1]
and
\varepsilon:=\gamma\mu
. Then

\lambda_{\max}=1-\frac{\delta+\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(\delta+\varepsilon)^{2}-4\varepsilon}.

Let
a:=\delta+\varepsilon
and
b:=4\varepsilon
. Under (
215
) the discriminant is nonnegative,
i.e.
0\leq b\leq a^{2}
, so we may use
\sqrt{a^{2}-b}\geq a-\frac{b}{a}
to obtain

\lambda_{\max}\;\geq\;1-\frac{a}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\Big(a-\frac{b}{a}\Big)=1-\frac{b}{2a}=1-\frac{2\varepsilon}{\delta+\varepsilon}\;\geq\;1-\frac{2\varepsilon}{\delta}=1-2\,\frac{\gamma\mu}{1-\beta}

which is (
216
) with a universal constant
c=2
. Since
A
is diagonalizable with eigenvalues in
(0,1)
, the solution of (
214
) can be written as a linear combination of
\lambda_{+}^{t}
and
\lambda_{-}^{t}
. For the symmetric initialization

\mathbb{E}[e_{0}]=\mathbb{E}[e_{-1}]=a
, the dominant mode is aligned with
\lambda_{\max}
, hence there exists a constant

C\geq 1
(depending only on the conditioning of the eigenbasis, and thus universal in the regime
(
215
)) such that (
217
) holds. The response time bound (
219
) follows immediately from the definition (
218
) and the
standard approximation
\log(1-x)\asymp-x
for
x\in(0,1/2)
, applied with
x=c\,\gamma\mu/(1-\beta)
. Finally, since
\phi_{u}(z_{t})-\phi_{u}(ua)=\frac{\mu}{2}e_{t}^{2}
and
\mathbb{E}[e_{t}^{2}]\geq(\mathbb{E}[e_{t}])^{2}
, plugging
(
217
) into this inequality yields (
220
).
∎

We now use this as the block length
\Delta_{T}\asymp\tau_{\beta}
. Since in our
J
-block construction, the minimizer is constant within a block and flips between blocks. If the blocks are much longer than
\tau_{\beta}
, (
SGDM
) has time to settle near the new minimizer after each switch. Then the regret per block is mostly transient and doesn’t accumulate strongly. On the other hand, if the block size is much shorter than
\tau_{\beta}
, (
SGDM
) will never be able to catch up. Taking
\Delta_{T}\asymp\tau_{\beta}
will give us a constant per-round suboptimality throughout the block (up to constants), hence resulting in
\Omega(\mu a^{2}\Delta_{T})
regret contribution per block. We formalize this in the following result:

Theorem E.1

(From response time to regret under block switching)
.

Consider the
J
-block construction of nonstationary losses
G^{u}_{1:T}
over
[T]
with blocks

B_{1},\dots,B_{J}
of lengths
|B_{j}|\in\{\Delta_{T},\Delta_{T}+1\}
,
\Delta_{T}=\lfloor T/J\rfloor
, and

G_{t}^{u}\equiv g_{u_{j}}
for
t\in B_{j}
, where
u\in\{\pm 1\}^{J}
. Assume the base losses are the translated
quadratics
\phi_{\pm}
above (embedded along
e_{1}
if
d>1
), so that within each block the unique minimizer
is
x^{\star}_{u_{j}}=u_{j}a
. Run Heavy-Ball (
SGDM
) with parameters
(\gamma,\beta)
satisfying the stability cap

\gamma\ \leq\ c_{0}\,\frac{(1-\beta)^{2}}{L},

(223)

for a universal constant
c_{0}>0
. Let
\tau_{\beta}
be the response time from
Proposition

E.1
,
so in particular

\tau_{\beta}\ \gtrsim\ \frac{L}{\mu(1-\beta)}.

(224)

Choose the block length on the order of the response time,

\Delta_{T}\ \asymp\ \tau_{\beta},\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad J\ \asymp\ \frac{T}{\tau_{\beta}}.

(225)

Then there exists a universal constant
c>0
such that for any
u\in\{\pm 1\}^{J}
with a sign flip at each
block boundary (e.g.,
u_{j+1}=-u_{j}
), the expected dynamic regret of Heavy-Ball (
SGDM
) satisfies

\mathcal{R}_{T}^{\pi_{\mathrm{HB}}}(G^{u})\;\geq\;c\,\mu a^{2}\,T.

(226)

Moreover, if the block family is tuned to satisfy the gradient-variation budget

\mathrm{GVar}_{p,q}(G^{u}_{1:T})\leq\mathbb{V}_{T}
via
Lemma

E.10
, i.e.

a\ \asymp\ \Big(\frac{\mathbb{V}_{T}}{\mu\,J^{1/q}}\Big)^{\!1/\alpha},\qquad\alpha:=1+\frac{d}{p},

(227)

then substituting
J\asymp T/\tau_{\beta}
into (
226
) yields

\mathcal{R}_{T}^{\pi_{\mathrm{HB}}}(G^{u})\;\gtrsim\;\mu^{1-2/\alpha}\,\mathbb{V}_{T}^{2/\alpha}\,\tau_{\beta}^{2/(\alpha q)}\,T^{1-(2/\alpha q)}.

(228)

Finally, using (
224
) gives the explicit inertia-dependent lower bound

\mathcal{R}_{T}^{\pi_{\mathrm{HB}}}(G^{u})\;\gtrsim\;\mu^{1-2/\alpha}\,\mathbb{V}_{T}^{2/\alpha}\,\Big(\frac{L}{\mu(1-\beta)}\Big)^{2/(\alpha q)}\,T^{1-(2/\alpha q)}.

(229)

Proof of
Theorem

E.1
.

The proof has three steps: (i) a single sign flip creates an

\Omega(a)
initialization error (in the extended state) relative to the
new
minimizer, (ii) over a time window
of length
\Theta(\tau_{\beta})
this error cannot contract by more than a constant factor, and (iii) strong convexity
converts this persistent distance into
\Omega(\mu a^{2})
per-round regret, which then accumulates across blocks.

Step 1: A flip induces an
\Omega(a)
error at the start of the next block.

Consider a boundary between two consecutive blocks
B_{j}
and
B_{j+1}
at which the sign flips,

u_{j+1}=-u_{j}
. Let

x^{\star}_{j}:=u_{j}a,\qquad x^{\star}_{j+1}:=u_{j+1}a=-u_{j}a,\qquad\text{so that}\qquad|x^{\star}_{j+1}-x^{\star}_{j}|=2a.

Let the first time index in block
B_{j+1}
be
t_{0}
and define the post-switch error variables

e_{t}:=x_{t}-x^{\star}_{j+1},\qquad t\in B_{j+1},

(and analogously for
e_{t_{0}-1}=x_{t_{0}-1}-x^{\star}_{j+1}
). By the reverse triangle inequality,

|e_{t_{0}-1}|=\big|x_{t_{0}-1}-x^{\star}_{j+1}\big|\geq|x^{\star}_{j}-x^{\star}_{j+1}|-\big|x_{t_{0}-1}-x^{\star}_{j}\big|=2a-\big|x_{t_{0}-1}-x^{\star}_{j}\big|.

(230)

Thus, whenever the iterate has achieved even a moderate accuracy on the previous block,
say
|x_{t_{0}-1}-x^{\star}_{j}|\leq a
, we obtain
|e_{t_{0}-1}|\geq a
. In particular, for the alternating-sign choice
in the theorem (a flip at every boundary), either the algorithm fails to track the previous minimizer by time

t_{0}-1
—which already incurs regret on block
B_{j}
—or else it necessarily starts block
B_{j+1}
with
initial error at least
a
relative to the new minimizer. This is what allows us to lower bound regret block-by-block. To make this quantitative in a way compatible with the Heavy-Ball state, define the extended state

s_{t}:=\begin{bmatrix}e_{t}\\
e_{t-1}\end{bmatrix}.

There exists a universal constant

c_{\mathrm{init}}>0
such that at the beginning of each block with a flip,

\|s_{t_{0}-1}\|_{2}\;\geq\;c_{\mathrm{init}}\,a.

(231)

We will use (
231
) as the initial condition for the lag argument in Step 2.

Step 2: Over
\Theta(\tau_{\beta})
steps the
mean
error cannot shrink by more than a constant factor.

On block
B_{j+1}
the loss is the fixed quadratic

g_{u_{j+1}}(x)=\frac{\mu}{2}(x-x^{\star}_{j+1})^{2}
, so with
e_{t}:=x_{t}-x^{\star}_{j+1}
the Heavy-Ball recursion gives

e_{t+1}=(1+\beta-\gamma\mu)e_{t}-\beta e_{t-1}-\gamma\eta_{t},\qquad t\in B_{j+1}.

Let
\bar{e}_{t}:=\mathbb{E}[e_{t}]
and
\bar{s}_{t}:=\begin{bmatrix}\bar{e}_{t}\\
\bar{e}_{t-1}\end{bmatrix}
. Taking expectations yields the
homogeneous linear system

\bar{s}_{t+1}=A\,\bar{s}_{t},\qquad A=\begin{bmatrix}1+\beta-\gamma\mu&-\beta\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}.

(232)

Fix a flipped boundary between
B_{j}
and
B_{j+1}
, and let
t_{0}
be the first index of
B_{j+1}
.
By Step 1, either the previous block already contributes
\gtrsim\mu a^{2}\Delta_{T}
regret (and we are done for that
block), or else the algorithm is at least moderately aligned with the previous minimizer in the sense that

|\mathbb{E}[x_{t_{0}-1}]-x^{\star}_{j}|\leq a
and
|\mathbb{E}[x_{t_{0}-2}]-x^{\star}_{j}|\leq a
.
In this latter case, using
x^{\star}_{j+1}=-x^{\star}_{j}
and the reverse triangle inequality for scalars gives

|\bar{e}_{t_{0}-1}|=|\mathbb{E}[x_{t_{0}-1}]-x^{\star}_{j+1}|\geq|x^{\star}_{j}-x^{\star}_{j+1}|-|\mathbb{E}[x_{t_{0}-1}]-x^{\star}_{j}|\geq 2a-a=a,

and similarly
|\bar{e}_{t_{0}-2}|\geq a
. Thus
\bar{s}_{t_{0}-1}
satisfies
|\bar{e}_{t_{0}-1}|,|\bar{e}_{t_{0}-2}|\geq a
.

Now apply
Proposition

E.1
to the shifted mean trajectory on this block (i.e., re-index time so that

t_{0}-1
plays the role of
0
). Using (
217
) and the definition (
218
), we obtain that there exists
a universal constant
c_{\mathrm{lag}}\in(0,1)
such that for all
k=0,1,\dots,\lfloor\tau_{\beta}\rfloor
,

|\bar{e}_{t_{0}+k}|\;\geq\;c_{\mathrm{lag}}\,a.

(233)

Consequently, Jensen implies that on the same window

\mathbb{E}[e_{t_{0}+k}^{2}]\ \geq\ (\mathbb{E}[e_{t_{0}+k}])^{2}\ \geq\ c_{\mathrm{lag}}^{\,2}\,a^{2}.

(234)

Step 3: Strong convexity turns persistent distance into regret and accumulates across blocks.

For the quadratic loss on block
B_{j+1}
,

g_{u_{j+1}}(x_{t})-g_{u_{j+1}}(x^{\star}_{j+1})=\frac{\mu}{2}\,e_{t}^{2}\;\geq\;\frac{\mu}{2}\,\Big(\,|e_{t}|\,\Big)^{2}.

(235)

By (
234
), for each
k\leq\lfloor c_{\mathrm{lag}}\tau_{\beta}\rfloor
we have

|e_{t_{0}+k}|\gtrsim a
, hence the per-round regret on that window is

g_{t_{0}+k}(x_{t_{0}+k})-g_{t_{0}+k}(x^{\star}_{t_{0}+k})\;=\;g_{u_{j+1}}(x_{t_{0}+k})-g_{u_{j+1}}(x^{\star}_{j+1})\;\gtrsim\;\mu a^{2}.

(236)

Summing (
236
) over
k=0,\dots,\lfloor c_{\mathrm{lag}}\tau_{\beta}\rfloor

gives an expected regret contribution of order
\mu a^{2}\tau_{\beta}
per flipped block:

\sum_{t\in B_{j+1}}\big(g_{t}(x_{t})-g_{t}(x_{t}^{\star})\big)\;\geq\;\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor c_{\mathrm{lag}}\tau_{\beta}\rfloor}\big(g_{t_{0}+k}(x_{t_{0}+k})-g_{t_{0}+k}(x^{\star}_{t_{0}+k})\big)\;\gtrsim\;\mu a^{2}\,\tau_{\beta}.

(237)

With the block-length choice
\Delta_{T}\asymp\tau_{\beta}
in (
225
), this becomes

\gtrsim\mu a^{2}\,\Delta_{T}
per block. Since the construction flips at every boundary, a constant fraction of the

J
blocks contribute this amount, and therefore

\mathcal{R}_{T}^{\pi_{\mathrm{HB}}}(F^{u})=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big(g_{t}(x_{t})-g_{t}(x_{t}^{\star})\big)\;\gtrsim\;J\cdot\mu a^{2}\,\Delta_{T}\;\asymp\;\mu a^{2}\,T,

which proves (
226
).

Under the
J
-block construction,
Lemma

E.10
gives

\mathrm{GVar}_{p,q}(G^{u}_{1:T})\lesssim\mu a^{\alpha}J^{1/q}
. Enforcing

\mathrm{GVar}_{p,q}(G^{u}_{1:T})\leq\mathbb{V}_{T}
yields (
227
) (absorbing constants).
Plugging (
227
) into (
226
) gives

\mathcal{R}_{T}^{\pi_{\mathrm{HB}}}(G^{u})\ \gtrsim\ \mu T\Big(\frac{\mathbb{V}_{T}}{\mu J^{1/q}}\Big)^{\!2/\alpha}=\mu^{1-2/\alpha}\,\mathbb{V}_{T}^{2/\alpha}\,T\,J^{-2/(\alpha q)}.

Using
J\asymp T/\tau_{\beta}
from (
225
) yields (
228
). Finally, the stability cap (
223
) implies
\gamma\lesssim(1-\beta)^{2}/L
, and combining this
with
\tau_{\beta}\asymp(1-\beta)/(\gamma\mu)
from
Proposition

E.1
yields

\tau_{\beta}\gtrsim L/(\mu(1-\beta))
, i.e. (
224
). Substituting this lower bound into
(
228
) gives (
229
).
∎

Since both the statistical and inertia constructions produce
\mu
-strongly convex,
\mu
-smooth functions satisfying
\mathrm{GVar}_{p,q}\leq\mathbb{V}_{T}
, the restricted minimax regret over
\Pi_{\beta}
is at least the maximum of the two lower bounds. This completes the proof of the result.

Appendix F
Additional experimental details and results

We provide complete details for reproducibility. All experiments compare SGD, Heavy-Ball (HB), and Nesterov (NAG) under identical drift conditions, with step sizes selected by a fixed tuning protocol described below.

F.1
General setup

Step size constraints and drift model.

Our results (
Theorem

3.3
and
Theorem

3.6
) assumes the sufficient stability condition

\gamma\leq\mu(1-\beta)^{2}/(4L^{2})
for HB/NAG to obtain clean finite-time bounds. In the main experimental setup, we
do not
enforce this conservative cap: we evaluate all methods at a standard set of base step sizes

\gamma
reported in the tables (and used uniformly across methods within each task/regime) to test whether the
drift-induced inertia phenomenon persists beyond the theoretically analyzed regime. For reference, when we choose
step sizes within the stability region, we observe the same qualitative behavior as
(
69
)
. Across all tasks, we evolve the population minimizer
\bm{\theta}_{t}^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
via a normalized random walk

\bm{\theta}_{t+1}^{\star}=\bm{\theta}_{t}^{\star}+\delta_{\mathrm{rw}}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{u}_{t}}{\|\mathbf{u}_{t}\|_{2}},\qquad\mathbf{u}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),

so that
\|\bm{\theta}_{t+1}^{\star}-\bm{\theta}_{t}^{\star}\|_{2}=\delta_{\mathrm{rw}}
for each step. We set

\delta_{\mathrm{rw}}=0.01
unless otherwise stated, and additionally report robustness to heavier-tailed Student-
t
drift
(
Table

3
).

Initialization and training.

All optimizers are initialized at
\bm{\theta}_{0}=\bm{\theta}_{0}^{\star}
with velocity buffer
\mathbf{v}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
, yielding zero initial tracking error to isolate the effect of drift. All experiments use mini-batch stochastic gradients with batch size
B=256
. The reported
\sigma^{2}
values refer to label-noise variance for the teacher–student MLP). We use fixed base step sizes
\gamma
for each task/regime (listed in the captions of
Table

3
and
Table

4
) rather than per-method tuning. For the teacher–student MLP, teacher and student weights are initialized i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,0.04)
, and the student is warm-started to match the teacher at
t=0
.

Evaluation.

We report the squared tracking error
e_{t}=\|\bm{\theta}_{t}-\bm{\theta}_{t}^{\star}\|^{2}
averaged over
N=20
runs, with final performance measured at
T=5000
iterations. Results were consistent across dimensions
d\in\{50,100,200\}
. The shaded regions in figures denote
\pm 1
standard deviation.

F.2
Task-specific details

Strongly convex quadratic.

We consider the time-varying objective

f_{t}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{\mu}{2}\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}_{t}^{\star}\|^{2},

(238)

with
\mu=1
, so that
f_{t}
is
1
-strongly convex with Lipschitz constant
L=\mu=1
(condition number
\kappa=1
). The gradient is
\nabla f_{t}(\bm{\theta})=\mu(\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}_{t}^{\star})
. At each iteration, the algorithm receives a stochastic gradient

G_{t}(\bm{\theta})=\nabla f_{t}(\bm{\theta})+\bm{\xi}_{t},\qquad\bm{\xi}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{d}),

(239)

with noise variance
\sigma^{2}\in\{0.1,0.5,0.8\}
. This task isolates the effect of gradient noise and momentum under drift in the simplest strongly convex setting.

Linear regression.

We consider streaming least-squares with drifting ground truth:

y_{t}=\langle\mathbf{x}_{t},\bm{\theta}_{t}^{\star}\rangle+\epsilon_{t},\qquad\epsilon_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}),

(240)

where covariates are drawn as
\mathbf{x}_{t}=\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbf{z}_{t}
with
\mathbf{z}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and
d=50
. The covariance
\Sigma
is constructed with eigenvalues log-spaced between
1
and
\kappa
, yielding strong convexity parameter
\mu=1
and smoothness
L=\kappa
. We test two conditioning regimes: well-conditioned (
\kappa=10
) and ill-conditioned (
\kappa=1000
).

Logistic regression.

We consider streaming binary classification with a drifting decision boundary:

y_{t}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}\bigl(\varsigma(\langle\mathbf{x}_{t},\bm{\theta}_{t}^{\star}\rangle)\bigr),

(241)

where
\varsigma(z)=1/(1+e^{-z})
is the sigmoid function. Covariates are generated identically to linear regression, with the same conditioning regimes (
\kappa\in\{10,1000\}
) and dimension
d=50
. A small
\ell_{2}
regularization term (
\lambda=10^{-3}
) is added to ensure strong convexity. We report tracking error
\|\bm{\theta}_{t}-\bm{\theta}_{t}^{\star}\|
, training loss, and classification accuracy evaluated on held-out samples.

Teacher–student MLP.

We consider a two-layer ReLU network of the form

f_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{W}_{2}\,\mathrm{ReLU}(\mathbf{W}_{1}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{b}_{1})+b_{2},

(242)

with architecture and training details summarized in
Table

2
. The teacher parameters
\bm{\theta}_{t}^{\star}=(\mathbf{W}_{1,t}^{\star},\mathbf{b}_{1,t}^{\star},\mathbf{W}_{2,t}^{\star},b_{2,t}^{\star})
drift according to the same normalized random walk described above. Minimizers are generated as
y_{t}=f_{\bm{\theta}_{t}^{\star}}(\mathbf{x}_{t})+\epsilon_{t}
with
\epsilon_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

Table 2
:
Teacher–student MLP architecture and training configuration.

d_{\mathrm{in}}
)

)

)

)

)

Seeds

20

Since parameter-space distances are not identifiable under the permutation and scaling symmetries of ReLU networks, we evaluate tracking in
prediction space
:

e_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{V}|}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{V}}\bigl\|f_{\bm{\theta}_{t}}(\mathbf{x})-f_{\bm{\theta}_{t}^{\star}}(\mathbf{x})\bigr\|^{2},

(243)

where
\mathcal{V}
is a fixed validation set held constant across all time steps. Conditioning is controlled via the input covariance
\Sigma
as in the regression tasks.
